   From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia
   American comedy musician (born 1959)
   For the album, see [188]"Weird Al" Yankovic (album).

   "Weird Al
   " Yankovic
   [189]"Weird Al" Yankovic in a multi-colored shirt, against a white
   background
   Yankovic at [190]GalaxyCon in 2024
   Background information
   Birth name Alfred Matthew Yankovic
   Born (1959-10-23) October 23, 1959 (age 65)
   [191]Downey, California, U.S.
   Origin [192]Lynwood, California, U.S.

   Discography [198]"Weird Al" Yankovic discography

   Musical artist

   Alfred Matthew "Weird Al" Yankovic ([206]/ˈjæŋkəvɪk/ ^[207]ⓘ
   [208]YANG-kə-vik;^[209][2] born October 23, 1959) is an American comedy
   musician, writer, and actor. He is best known for writing and
   performing [210]comedy songs that often [211]parody specific songs by
   contemporary musicians. He also performs original songs that are style
   [212]pastiches of the work of other acts, as well as [213]polka medleys
   of several popular songs, most of which feature his trademark
   [214]accordion.

   Since having one of his comedy songs aired on [215]The Dr. Demento
   Radio Show in 1976 at age 16, Yankovic has sold more than 12 million
   albums (as of 2015^[216][update]),^[217][3]^[218][4]^[[219]needs
   update] recorded more than 150 parodies and original
   songs,^[220][5]^[221][6]^[222][7] and performed more than 1,000 live
   shows.^[223][8] His work has earned him five [224]Grammy Awards and a
   further 11 nominations, four [225]gold records and six [226]platinum
   records in the U.S. His first top ten [227]Billboard album
   ([228]Straight Outta Lynwood) and single ("[229]White & Nerdy") were
   both released in 2006, nearly three decades into his career. His
   fourteenth studio album, [230]Mandatory Fun (2014), became his first
   number-one album during its debut week.

   Yankovic's success has been attributed to his effective use of music
   videos to further parody pop culture, the song's original artist, and
   the original music videos themselves. He has directed some of his own
   music videos and has also directed music videos for other artists
   including [231]Ben Folds, [232]Hanson, [233]the Black Crowes, and
   [234]the Presidents of the United States of America. With the decline
   of music television and the onset of social media, he used YouTube and
   other video sites to publish his videos; this strategy helped boost
   sales of his later albums. He has not released a full album since
   Mandatory Fun, opting instead for timely releases of singles.

   In addition to his music career, Yankovic wrote and starred in the film
   [235]UHF (1989) and the television series [236]The Weird Al Show
   (1997). He has produced two satirical films about his own life,
   [237]The Compleat Al (1985) and [238]Weird: The Al Yankovic Story
   (2022). He has acted in several television shows and web series, in
   addition to starring in [239]Al TV specials on [240]MTV. He has also
   written two children's books, When I Grow Up (2011) and My New Teacher
   and Me! (2013).

Early life

                             External videos
   video icon [241]'Weird Al' Yankovic – Wikipedia: Fact or Fiction?,
   7:02, [242]Diffuser.fm^[243][9]

   Alfred Matthew Yankovic was born in [244]Downey, California, on October
   23, 1959,^[245][10] the only child of Mary Elizabeth (née Vivalda,
   1923–2004) and Nick Yankovic (1917–2004). He was raised in nearby
   [246]Lynwood, California.^[247][11] His father, who was born in the
   [248]Strawberry Hill neighborhood of [249]Kansas City, Kansas, was of
   [250]Slovene and [251]Croatian descent: Nick Yankovic was the son of
   Matthew Yankovich (baptized Matija Jankovič, 1887–1969), who was born
   in [252]Bedenj, [253]Slovenia,^[254][11]^[255][12]^[256][13] and Mary
   Yankovich (née Braj, 1890–1968), born in Croatia.^[257][14] Nick
   Yankovic began living in California after earning two [258]Purple
   Hearts for his service as a medic during [259]World War
   II.^[260][15]^[261][16]^[262][17] He believed "the key to success" was
   "doing for a living whatever makes you happy" and often reminded his
   son of this philosophy.^[263][15] Yankovic's mother, a
   [264]stenographer from [265]Kentucky of [266]English and [267]Italian
   descent, married his father in 1949. She moved to California a decade
   before Yankovic was born.^[268][15]^[269][17]

   Yankovic's first accordion lesson, which sparked his interest in music,
   took place on the day before his seventh birthday.^[270][17] A
   door-to-door salesman traveling through Lynwood offered his parents a
   choice of accordion or guitar lessons at a local music school. Yankovic
   claims that his parents chose the accordion over the guitar because
   "they figured there should be at least one more accordion-playing
   Yankovic in the world"; this was in reference to [271]Frankie
   Yankovic,^[272][18]^[273][17] to whom he is not related.^[274][13] He
   has also said that they chose the accordion because "they were
   convinced it would revolutionize rock".^[275][13] Since his mother did
   not let him outside the house often, he had plenty of time to practice
   the instrument at home.^[276][17] He continued lessons at the school
   for three years before deciding to continue learning on his
   own.^[277][11]

   In the 1970s, Yankovic was a big fan of [278]Elton John and cites
   John's 1973 album [279]Goodbye Yellow Brick Road as one of the reasons
   he "learned to play rock 'n roll on the accordion".^[280][15] As for
   his influences in comedy and parody music, he has listed artists
   including [281]Stan Freberg, [282]Spike Jones, [283]Tom Lehrer,
   [284]Allan Sherman, [285]Shel Silverstein and [286]Frank Zappa as well
   as "all the other wonderfully sick and twisted artists" he found
   through [287]The Dr. Demento Radio Show.^[288][11]^[289][19] Other
   sources of inspiration for his comedy came from [290]Mad
   magazine,^[291][15] the British comedy troupe [292]Monty
   Python,^[293][20] and the [294]Zucker, Abrahams and Zucker
   films.^[295][21] He had also enjoyed [296]George Carlin's stand-up
   comedy album [297]FM & AM so much that he transcribed it by
   typewriter.^[298][17]
   [299][220px-Lynwood_High_School_yearbook_%22Accolade_1976%22%2C_page_13
   9_%28cropped_to_%22Weird_Al%22_Yankovic%29.jpg] Yankovic in his Lynwood
   High School yearbook, Accolade 1976

   Yankovic began kindergarten a year earlier than most children and
   skipped [300]second grade, later saying, "My classmates seemed to think
   I was some kind of rocket scientist, so I was labeled a nerd early
   on."^[301][15] He attended [302]Lynwood High School, where his unusual
   schooling experience meant he was two years younger than most of his
   classmates. He was not interested in sports or social events but was
   active in other extracurricular programs, including the [303]National
   Forensic League-sanctioned public speaking events; a play based on
   [304]Rebel Without a Cause; the yearbook, for which he wrote most of
   the captions; and the Volcano Worshippers club, which he later said did
   "absolutely nothing" and was started "just to get an extra picture of
   [themselves] in the yearbook".^[305][15] He graduated in
   1976,^[306][22] and was [307]valedictorian of his senior
   class.^[308][15] He attended [309]California Polytechnic State
   University in [310]San Luis Obispo, earning a bachelor's degree in
   architecture.^[311][13] After graduation he worked at [312]Westwood
   One, first in the mail room and then calling stations confirming that
   paid advertisements had indeed run on air.^[313][23]

Career

1976–1981: Dr. Demento and early fame

   Yankovic received his first exposure via syndicated comedy radio
   personality [314]Dr. Demento's Southern California–based radio show,
   later saying, "If there hadn't been a Dr. Demento, I'd probably have a
   real job now."^[315][24] Despite his mother having caught him listening
   to Dr. Demento's program and banning him from listening to it again, he
   found ways to hear it discreetly.^[316][17] In 1976, Dr. Demento spoke
   at Yankovic's school, where the 16-year-old Yankovic gave him a
   homemade [317]cassette tape of original and parody songs performed on
   the accordion in Yankovic's bedroom into a "cheesy little tape
   recorder". The tape's first song, "Belvedere Cruisin'" (about his
   family's [318]Plymouth Belvedere) was played on Demento's comedy radio
   show, launching Yankovic's career. Demento said, "'Belvedere Cruising'
   might not have been the very best song I ever heard, but it had some
   clever lines [...] I put the tape on the air
   immediately."^[319][15]^[320][25] Yankovic also played at local
   coffeehouses, accompanied by fellow dorm resident Joel Miller on
   bongos.^[321][26] He recalled in 2007:

     It was sort of like amateur music night, and a lot of people were
     like wannabe [322]Dan Fogelbergs. They'd get up on stage with their
     acoustic guitar and do these lovely ballads. And I would get up with
     my accordion and play [323]the theme from 2001. And people were kind
     of shocked that I would be disrupting their mellow Thursday night
     folk fest.^[324][27]

   During Yankovic's second year as an architecture student at California
   Polytechnic State University, San Luis Obispo, he became a disc jockey
   at [325]KCPR, the university's radio station. Yankovic had been called
   "Weird Al" originally as a more derogatory nickname from others within
   the dormitory he shared, as he was seen as the strange outcast compared
   to other residents.^[326][17] Though he initially took it as an insult,
   Yankovic eventually "took it on professionally" as his persona for the
   station.^[327][15] In 1978, he released his first recording (as Alfred
   Yankovic), "Take Me Down", on the LP Slo Grown, as a benefit for the
   Economic Opportunity Commission of [328]San Luis Obispo County. The
   song mocked famous nearby landmarks such as [329]Bubblegum Alley and
   the waterfall toilets at the [330]Madonna Inn.

   In mid-1979, shortly before his senior year, "[331]My Sharona" by
   [332]the Knack was on the charts, and Yankovic took his accordion into
   the restroom across the hall from the radio station to take advantage
   of the [333]echo chamber acoustics and recorded a parody titled
   "[334]My Bologna".^[335][28] He sent it to Dr. Demento, who played it
   to good response from listeners. Yankovic met the Knack after a show at
   his college and introduced himself as the author of "My Bologna". The
   Knack's lead singer, [336]Doug Fieger, said he liked the song and
   suggested that [337]Capitol Records vice president [338]Rupert Perry
   release it as a single.^[339][15] "My Bologna" was released as a single
   with "School Cafeteria" as its B-side, and the label gave Yankovic a
   six-month recording contract. Yankovic, who was "only getting average
   grades" in his architecture degree, began to realize that he might make
   a career of comedic music.^[340][15]

   On September 14, 1980, Yankovic was a guest on the Dr. Demento Show,
   where he was to record a new parody live. The song was called
   "[341]Another One Rides the Bus", a parody of [342]Queen's hit
   "[343]Another One Bites the Dust". While practicing the song outside
   the sound booth, he met [344]Jon "Bermuda" Schwartz, who told him he
   was a drummer and agreed to bang on Yankovic's accordion case to help
   Yankovic keep a steady beat during the song. They rehearsed the song
   just a few times before the show began.^[345][15] "Another One Rides
   the Bus" became so popular that Yankovic's first television appearance
   was a performance of the song on [346]The Tomorrow Show with [347]Tom
   Snyder on April 21, 1981.^[348][29]^[349][30]^[350][31] On the show,
   Yankovic played his accordion, and again, Schwartz banged on the
   accordion case and provided comical sound effects. Yankovic's record
   label, [351]TK Records, went bankrupt about two weeks after the single
   was released, so Yankovic received no royalties from its initial
   release.^[352][28]

1981–1989: Band and fame

   [353][220px-Weirdalclassic.jpg] Yankovic's "classic" look before eye
   surgery: with glasses, mustache and short, curly hair; used from 1979
   to 1998[354][220px-Weird_Al_Yankovic_and_Tom_Griffin.jpg] Yankovic
   speaking with a concert promoter before a show in 1984

   1981 brought Yankovic on tour for the first time as part of Dr.
   Demento's stage show. His stage act in a [355]Phoenix, Arizona,
   nightclub caught the eye of manager Jay Levey, who was "blown
   away".^[356][15] Levey asked Yankovic if he had considered creating a
   full band and doing his music as a career. Yankovic admitted that he
   had, so Levey held auditions. [357]Steve Jay became Yankovic's bass
   player, and Jay's friend [358]Jim West played guitar. Schwartz
   continued on drums. Yankovic's first show with his new band was on
   March 31, 1982.^[359][8] Several days later, Yankovic and his band were
   the opening act for [360]Missing Persons.

   Yankovic recorded "[361]I Love Rocky Road" (a parody of "[362]I Love
   Rock 'n' Roll" originally recorded by [363]The Arrows), which was
   produced by [364]Rick Derringer, in 1982. The song was a hit on Top 40
   radio, leading to Yankovic's signing with [365]Scotti Brothers Records.
   In 1983, Yankovic's first [366]self-titled album was released on Scotti
   Bros. The song "[367]Ricky" (a parody of [368]Toni Basil's hit
   "[369]Mickey") was released as a single and the music video received
   exposure on the still-young [370]MTV. "Ricky" broke the top 100 videos
   on MTV at the time, which Yankovic took as a sign that his career was
   in music, quitting his job as a mailroom clerk at the local offices of
   [371]Westwood One to pursue the music career.^[372][26]

   Yankovic released his second album [373]"Weird Al" Yankovic in 3-D in
   1984. The first single "[374]Eat It", a parody of the [375]Michael
   Jackson song "[376]Beat It", became popular, thanks in part to the
   music video, a shot-for-shot parody of Jackson's "Beat It" music video,
   and what Yankovic sarcastically described as his "uncanny resemblance"
   to Jackson.^[377][17] Yankovic said he felt he had become an overnight
   success once the video for "Eat It" aired on [378]MTV, as both the song
   and video, as well as Jackson's approval for the parody, drew attention
   to him from other musicians and made it easier for him to obtain
   permissions to use others' songs.^[379][32] "Eat It" was also aided by
   the first of Yankovic's [380]Al TV specials that aired on [381]MTV on
   April 1, 1984, the network looking to Yankovic's rising popularity to
   help fill its programming time.^[382][17] Peaking at No. 12 on the
   [383]Billboard [384]Hot 100 on April 14, 1984,^[385][33] "Eat It"
   remained Yankovic's highest-charting single until "[386]White & Nerdy"
   placed at No. 9 in October 2006.^[387][34] In Canada, "Eat It" reached
   No. 5.^[388][35]

   In 1985, Yankovic co-wrote and starred in a [389]mockumentary of his
   own life titled [390]The Compleat Al (the title being a parody of the
   1982 documentary [391]The Compleat Beatles), which intertwined the
   facts of his life up to that point with fiction. The film also featured
   some clips from Yankovic's trip to Japan and some clips from the Al TV
   specials. The Compleat Al was co-directed by Jay Levey, who would
   direct [392]UHF four years later. Also released around the same time as
   The Compleat Al was The Authorized Al, a biographical book based on the
   film. The book, resembling a scrapbook, included real and fictional
   humorous photographs and documents.

   Yankovic and his band toured as the opening act for [393]the Monkees in
   mid-1987 for their second reunion tour of North America. Yankovic
   claims to have enjoyed touring with the Monkees, even though "the
   promoter gypped us out of a bunch of money".^[394][36]

   In 1988, Yankovic was the narrator on the [395]Wendy Carlos recording
   of [396]Sergei Prokofiev's [397]Peter and the Wolf.^[398][37] The album
   also included a sequel to [399]Camille Saint-Saëns's composition
   [400]The Carnival of the Animals, titled "The Carnival of the Animals
   Part II", with Yankovic providing humorous poems for each of the
   featured creatures in the style of [401]Ogden Nash, who had written
   humorous poems for the original.

   Yankovic's success led to a deal to make his film UHF, which premiered
   in July 1989. While the film has since become a cult title, its initial
   release was against mediocre reviews, and it was up against several
   other summer blockbusters, including [402]Indiana Jones and the Last
   Crusade, [403]Ghostbusters II, [404]Batman, and [405]Licence to Kill.
   While Yankovic released an associated soundtrack album, [406]UHF –
   Original Motion Picture Soundtrack and Other Stuff, it was not as
   successful as his previous albums. Yankovic fell into a slump over the
   next three years as a result of the poor performance of the
   film.^[407][38]

1990–1997: Revived career

   Yankovic had returned to the studio to prepare songs for his next album
   [408]Off the Deep End around 1990. During production, [409]Rubén
   Valtierra joined the band on keyboards in 1991, allowing Yankovic to
   concentrate more on singing and increasing his use of the stage space
   during concerts. Further, Yankovic took over production from [410]Rick
   Derringer in 1992. While Derringer had produced six of Yankovic's
   previous albums, for which he won two [411]Grammy Awards, Derringer's
   drug-related issues had become a problem, along with Yankovic's
   increasingly more complex musical vision (involving horns and other
   instruments).^[412][26]

   By 1992, most of the original songs for Off the Deep End were complete,
   but Yankovic still did not have a strong parody and was waiting for the
   next big hit to work from, as he was still in a slump post-UHF. When
   Jackson released his next album, [413]Dangerous, and its hit single
   "[414]Black or White", Yankovic had quickly written a parody, "Snack
   All Night", from it, and hoped Jackson would allow him to use the
   parody. Jackson denied Yankovic this, as Jackson felt "Black or White"
   carried a serious message that would be undermined by the
   parody.^[415][39] Again, Yankovic fell into a mood and delayed release
   of Off the Deep End without a lead parody. Around this time,
   [416]Nirvana and the grunge music scene began to take off. Yankovic
   wrote a parody of Nirvana's hit "[417]Smells Like Teen Spirit",
   "[418]Smells Like Nirvana", and was able to secure the band's
   permission for the parody; Nirvana's lead singer [419]Kurt Cobain
   reportedly said that getting Yankovic to parody their work was a sign
   their band had "made it".^[420][40] "Smells Like Nirvana" became the
   lead song on Off the Deep End, landing at No. 35 on the Billboard
   charts, his second top 40 hit in the United States. Off the Deep End
   reached No. 17 on the Billboard 200, and helped to revitalize
   Yankovic's career after the failure of UHF.

   Yankovic's next two studio albums were modest successes in light of Off
   the Deep End. [421]Alapalooza was released in 1993, and led with
   "[422]Jurassic Park", a spoof of "[423]MacArthur Park" by [424]Richard
   Harris while mocking the [425]1993 film of the same name. Alapalooza
   peaked at No. 46 on the Billboard 200. [426]Bad Hair Day in 1996
   headlined with "[427]Amish Paradise", a parody of [428]Coolio's
   "[429]Gangsta's Paradise". "Amish Paradise" reached No. 53 on the top
   Billboard 100 singles, while the album reached No. 14 on the Billboard
   200, and eventually was certified Double Platinum in sales by RIAA,
   making it one of Yankovic's more successful works.

   In addition, Yankovic released a number of compilation works during
   this period, including [430]Permanent Record: Al in the Box, a four-CD
   collection which included most of Yankovic's previous works as well as
   an informational booklet with contributions from Dr. Demento. Other
   compilations included [431]Greatest Hits Volume II, a collection of
   songs that were not included in Permanent Record, and [432]The TV
   Album, featuring songs loosely based on television shows.

1998–2014: New look

   On January 24, 1998, Yankovic had [433]LASIK eye surgery to correct his
   extreme [434]myopia.^[435][41] Yankovic had the surgery for free when
   he agreed to let [436]KTLA Morning News broadcast it live on
   television.^[437][42] When [438]Running with Scissors debuted in 1999,
   he unveiled a radically changed look. In addition to shedding his
   glasses, he had shaved off his moustache and grown out his hair. He had
   previously shaved his moustache in 1983 for the video of "[439]Ricky"
   to resemble [440]Desi Arnaz, in 1989 for segments of the "[441]UHF"
   music video and in 1996 for the "Amish Paradise" video. Yankovic
   reasoned, "If [442]Madonna's allowed to reinvent herself every
   15 minutes, I figure I should be good for a change at least once every
   20 years."^[443][43] He parodied the reaction to this "new look" in a
   commercial for his nonexistent [444]MTV Unplugged special. The
   commercial featured Yankovic in the short-haired wig from the music
   video for [445]Hanson's "River", claiming his new look was an attempt
   to "get back to the core of what I'm all about", that being "the
   music".^[446][44]

   Running with Scissors was followed by his next studio album [447]Poodle
   Hat in 2003. Poodle Hat was met with average reviews without any
   standout singles, though the album did peak at number 17 on the
   [448]Billboard 200. Yankovic's following album was [449]Straight Outta
   Lynwood in 2006, which featured the single "White & Nerdy", a parody of
   "[450]Ridin'" by [451]Chamillionaire. "White & Nerdy" became Yankovic's
   first Billboard Top Ten single, debuting at No. 29 and peaking at No.
   9. "[452]Canadian Idiot", a parody of "[453]American Idiot" by
   [454]Green Day, also charted in the Hot 100. The album as a whole
   reached No. 10 in the Billboard 200, and by 2008 was Yankovic's first
   [455]certified platinum album, having reached over one million sales.

   Following Straight Out of Lynwood, Yankovic started to explore
   [456]digital distribution of his songs. On October 7, 2008, Yankovic
   released to the [457]iTunes Store "[458]Whatever You Like", a parody of
   the [459]T.I. [460]song of the same title, which Yankovic said he had
   come up with two weeks before. Yankovic said that the benefit of
   digital distribution is that "I don't have to wait around while my
   songs get old and dated—I can get them out on the Internet almost
   immediately."^[461][45] In 2009, Yankovic released four more songs:
   "[462]Craigslist" on June 16, "[463]Skipper Dan" on July 14, "[464]CNR"
   on August 4, and "[465]Ringtone" on August 25. These five digitally
   released songs were packaged as a digital EP titled [466]Internet
   Leaks, with "Whatever You Like" retroactively included in the
   set.^[467][46]

   In 2011, Yankovic completed his thirteenth studio album, titled
   [468]Alpocalypse, which was released on June 21, 2011.^[469][47] The
   album contains the five songs from the previous [470]Internet Leaks
   digital download release, a polka medley called "Polka Face", a song
   called "TMZ", for which [471]Bill Plympton created an animated music
   video, and five other new songs.^[472][48]^[473][49]

   Yankovic had reported an interest in parodying [474]Lady Gaga's
   material,^[475][50] and on April 20 announced that he had written and
   recorded a parody of "[476]Born This Way" titled "[477]Perform This
   Way" to be the lead single for his new album. However, upon first
   submitting it to Lady Gaga's manager for approval (which Yankovic does
   as a courtesy), he was not given permission to release it
   commercially.^[478][51] As he had previously done under similar
   circumstances (with his parody of James Blunt's "[479]You're
   Beautiful", which was titled "[480]You're Pitiful"), Yankovic then
   released the song for free on the internet. Soon afterwards, Gaga's
   manager admitted that he had denied the parody of his own accord
   without forwarding the song to his client, and upon seeing it online,
   Lady Gaga granted permission for the parody.^[481][52] Yankovic has
   stated that all of his proceeds from the parody and its music video
   will be donated to the [482]Human Rights Campaign, to support the human
   rights themes of the original song. Yankovic was also a judge for the
   10th annual Independent Music Awards to support independent artists'
   careers.^[483][53]

   Yankovic stated in September 2013 that he was working on a new
   album,^[484][54] and in 2014, he used social media websites to hint at
   a July 15 release of the new album.^[485][55] The album artwork and
   title, [486]Mandatory Fun, were confirmed by his publisher.^[487][56]
   Mandatory Fun was released to strong critical praise and was the No. 1
   debut album on the Billboard charts the week of its release, buoyed by
   Yankovic's approach for releasing eight music videos over eight
   continuous days that drew viral attention to the album as described
   below.^[488][57] It became Yankovic's first No. 1 album in his career.
   Additionally, the song "[489]Word Crimes" (a parody of [490]Robin
   Thicke's "[491]Blurred Lines") reached No. 39 on the Top 100 singles
   for the same week; this is Yankovic's fourth Top 40 single (following
   "Eat It", "Smells Like Nirvana", and "White & Nerdy") and made him the
   third musical artist, after [492]Michael Jackson and Madonna, to have a
   Top 40 single in each decade since the 1980s.^[493][58]

2015–present: Transition from studio albums

   Yankovic said in an interview promoting Mandatory Fun that, with the
   end of his recording contract, it was likely his last traditional
   album, in the sense of recording and releasing that many songs at a
   time; he said he will likely switch to releasing singles and EPs over
   the Internet, a method which offers more immediate release
   opportunities, as Yankovic considers his parodies in particular as
   something that can become dated by the time of release.^[494][59] Since
   Mandatory Fun, Yankovic has not released any additional albums. In a
   2017 interview with [495]Rolling Stone, Yankovic said, "I can't tell
   you when any material is coming out. Inspiration could strike tomorrow
   and I might have something out next month. There's no plan. It's just
   going to be whenever it winds up being."^[496][60]

   After several years of fan-driven campaigns, Yankovic received his own
   star on the [497]Hollywood Walk of Fame in 2018.^[498][61]^[499][62]

   In March 2018, Yankovic released a new song, "The Hamilton Polka", a
   polka medley consisting of several songs from the musical
   [500]Hamilton, which were written by [501]Lin-Manuel
   Miranda.^[502][63]^[503][64] Yankovic and Miranda had met and became
   friends prior to the latter's work on Hamilton and had discussed a
   possible music project together. After the success of Hamilton, Miranda
   provided Yankovic the score from the musical, hoping that Yankovic
   would create a few singles from it, but Yankovic ultimately created a
   full polka medley from it.^[504][65] The song holds the distinction of
   being the first polka song to chart on Billboard's [505]Digital Songs
   Sales Chart.^[506][66] After Hamilton had premiered on [507]Disney+ in
   July 2020, Yankovic released a video version of "The Hamilton Polka"
   that synched his song to video clips from the show.^[508][67] Also in
   March, Yankovic released two [509]remixes of songs by [510]Portugal.
   The Man: "[511]Feel It Still" and "[512]Live in the Moment".^[513][68]
   In 2020, he collaborated with the band again on their single "Who's
   Gonna Stop Me", which was released for [514]Indigenous Peoples'
   Day.^[515][69]

   Following the release of Mandatory Fun, Yankovic has not recorded many
   new songs, instead focusing on projects such as his tours, and the 2022
   parody biopic [516]Weird: The Al Yankovic Story. That same year,
   Yankovic told the [517]Los Angeles Times that he was not writing many
   new parodies because it had become harder to tell which new songs would
   be big hits, due to what the newspaper described as popular music
   trending more towards "micro-niche[s]" than a "monoculture".^[518][70]
   Yankovic released a new polka medley, "Polkamania!", on July 19, 2024,
   featuring his take on "[519]Flowers", "[520]Bad Guy", and "[521]Old
   Town Road", among others. Because he had not released a polka in ten
   years, he considered this the opportunity to include his "white
   whales", though limited the songs to those that reach #1 on the
   Billboard charts.^[522][71]^[523][72] The single included the release
   of video created by several animators that Yankovic had worked with in
   past videos, such as [524]Bill Plympton, [525]Augenblick Studios,
   [526]Liam Lynch, and Jarrett Heather as well as new collaborations with
   artists like [527]Cyriak and [528]Vivienne Medrano.^[529][73]^[530][74]

Personal life

   Yankovic became a vegetarian in 1992 when his girlfriend gave him a
   copy of the 1987 [531]John Robbins book [532]Diet for a New America,
   which he said "made a very compelling argument for a strict vegetarian
   diet".^[533][75]^[534][76] When asked how he can rationalize performing
   shows at events such as the Great American Rib Cook-Off as a
   vegetarian, he replied, "The same way I can rationalize playing at a
   college even though I'm not a student anymore."^[535][77] In a 2011
   interview with [536]OnMilwaukee, he clarified his stance on his diet,
   "I am still a vegetarian, and I try to be a [537]vegan, but I
   occasionally cheat. If there's a cheese pizza on the band bus, I might
   sneak a piece."^[538][78]

   In 1998, Yankovic underwent [539]LASIK (Laser-Assisted in Situ
   Keratomileusis) eye surgery to correct his near-sightedness. As his
   glasses were part of his signature look prior to the surgery, Yankovic
   considered wearing fake glasses but decided against it. Around this
   time, he had also decided to shave off his trademark
   mustache.^[540][79]

   Yankovic married Suzanne Krajewski, a marketing executive with
   [541]20th Century Fox, after they met in 2001. They were introduced to
   each other on a [542]blind date by their mutual friend [543]Bill
   Mumy.^[544][76]^[545][17] Their daughter, Nina, was born in
   2003.^[546][80] They live in Los Angeles, where they own a house
   previously owned by writer [547]Jack S. Margolis and rapper [548]Heavy
   D.^[549][81] In contrast to his stage persona, Yankovic is known by
   friends and associates to be polite, shy, and introverted, even among
   family.^[550][17] He is a Christian, and a married couple from the
   church he attends can be seen in the background on the cover of his
   album Poodle Hat.^[551][82]^[552][83] His religious upbringing is
   reflected in his [553]abstinence from profanity, alcohol, and
   drugs.^[554][84]

   On April 9, 2004, Yankovic's parents were found dead at their home in
   [555]Fallbrook, California, the victims of accidental [556]carbon
   monoxide poisoning from their fireplace.^[557][16]^[558][80] Hours
   after his wife notified him of this, he made the decision to go on with
   his concert in [559]Appleton, Wisconsin.^[560][85]^[561][86] He later
   said, "Since my music had helped many of my fans through tough times,
   maybe it would work for me as well ... it would at least give me a
   break from sobbing all the time."^[562][87]^[563][88] Their deaths
   occurred following the release of Poodle Hat, which was Yankovic's
   lowest-selling album in 20 years. He considered the Appleton show and
   subsequent tour dates therapeutic: "If I didn't have anything to
   distract me, I probably would have spiraled into an even deeper
   depression. For a couple of hours each night, I could go onstage and
   put on a big fake smile and pretend like everything was just
   okay."^[564][81] In a 2014 interview, he cited the deaths of his
   parents as the worst thing that had ever happened to him, adding, "I
   knew intellectually, that at some point, probably, I'd have to, you
   know, live through the death of my parents, but I never thought it
   would be at the same time, and so abruptly."^[565][89]

Musical style

   Main article: [566]List of songs recorded by "Weird Al" Yankovic

Parodies

   [567][170px-Kyle-cassidy-weird-al-yankovic.jpg] Yankovic, photographed
   by [568]Kyle Cassidy

   Yankovic is well known for creating parodies of contemporary radio
   hits, which typically make up about half of his studio releases. Unlike
   other parody artists such as [569]Allan Sherman, Yankovic and his band
   strive to keep the backing music in his parodies the same as the
   original, transcribing the original song by ear and re-recording the
   song for the parody.^[570][90] In some cases, after Yankovic has
   requested that the original band allow his parody, the band will offer
   to help out with the recreation: [571]Dire Straits members [572]Mark
   Knopfler and [573]Guy Fletcher perform on "[574]Money for
   Nothing/Beverly Hillbillies*", Yankovic's parody of [575]Dire Straits'
   "[576]Money for Nothing", while [577]Imagine Dragons provided Yankovic
   with advice on how to recreate some of the electronic sounds they used
   for "Radioactive" in Yankovic's parody "Inactive".^[578][91] Yankovic's
   career in novelty and comedy music has outlasted many of his
   "mainstream" parody targets, such as [579]Toni Basil, [580]MC Hammer,
   and [581]Men Without Hats.^[582][92]^[583][93] Yankovic's continued
   success (including the top 10 single "White & Nerdy" and album
   [584]Straight Outta Lynwood in 2006) has enabled him to escape the
   one-hit wonder stigma often associated with novelty music.^[585][94]

   Yankovic considers his body of work to primarily feature parodies,
   rather than satires of the original song or artist, as he found that
   satire of songs or artists has already been done before.^[586][95] Most
   Yankovic songs consist of the original song's music, with a separate,
   unrelated set of amusing lyrics. Yankovic's humor normally lies more in
   creating unexpected incongruity between an artist's image and the topic
   of the song, contrasting the style of the song with its content (such
   as the songs "Amish Paradise", "White & Nerdy", and "You're Pitiful"),
   or in pointing out trends or works which have become pop culture
   clichés (such as "[587]eBay" and "[588]Don't Download This Song").
   Yankovic's parodies are often satirical of popular culture, including
   television (see [589]The TV Album), films ("[590]The Saga Begins"), and
   food (see [591]The Food Album). Yankovic claims he has no intention of
   writing "serious" music. In his reasoning, "There's enough people that
   do unfunny music. I'll leave the serious stuff to [592]Paris Hilton and
   [593]Kevin Federline."^[594][96]

   Yankovic considered that his first true satirical song was "Smells Like
   Nirvana", which references unintelligible lyrics in Nirvana's
   "[595]Smells Like Teen Spirit".^[596][97] Other satirical songs include
   "[597]Achy Breaky Song", which refers to the song "[598]Achy Breaky
   Heart", "(This Song's Just) Six Words Long", which refers to the
   repetitious lyrics in "[599]Got My Mind Set on You", and "Perform This
   Way", set to [600]Lady Gaga's "[601]Born This Way", that drew
   inspiration from Lady Gaga's outlandish but confident
   attitude.^[602][95]

   Yankovic is the sole writer for all his songs and, for "legal and
   personal reasons", does not accept parody submissions or ideas from
   fans.^[603][11] There exists, however, one exception to this rule:
   Madonna was reportedly talking with a friend and rhetorically asked
   when Yankovic was going to turn her "[604]Like a Virgin" into
   "[605]Like a Surgeon". Madonna's friend was a mutual friend of
   Yankovic's manager, Jay Levey, and eventually Yankovic himself heard
   the story from Levey.^[606][15] In writing his parodies as well as his
   original songs, Yankovic spends a great deal of time in deciding the
   right words that not only match the beat of the original song but that
   fit the theme of the parody. He says that the lyrics of some songs have
   taken him weeks to write as he permutes the various choices, sometimes
   entering a "zombie phase" as he mulls these over in his home. For
   example, Yankovic believes he could have written a completely different
   version of "White & Nerdy" based on the alternative choices of lyrics
   he had come up with and had discarded for the final song. He has also
   done significant research for other song parodies to get facts and
   keywords for certain areas of knowledge, such as for "I Think I'm a
   Clone Now" or hospitals for "Like a Surgeon". Yankovic has documented
   all these past lyrical attempts, first through binders and then
   computerized in case he needs to go back for future songs.^[607][17]

Polka medleys

   Main article: [608]List of "Weird Al" Yankovic polka medleys

   Most of Yankovic's studio albums include a [609]polka [610]medley of
   about a dozen contemporary songs at the time of the album, with the
   choruses or memorable lines of various songs juxtaposed for humorous
   effect. In Yankovic's early career, before recording his first album,
   he had performed such polka medleys in live shows in California, though
   then using songs from lesser-known bands like [611]Bad Brains and the
   [612]Plasmatics. He had been inspired to do so from Spike Jones, who
   had transitioned from classical music into polka.^[613][98] Yankovic
   said that converting these songs to polka was "...the way God
   intended".^[614][99] Yankovic did not include a medley on his first
   album, but considered this for his second, In 3-D, recognizing that it
   would only work if he used well-known songs. The resulting "Polkas on
   45", which featured songs from [615]Devo, [616]Deep Purple,
   [617]Berlin, and [618]The Beatles, was popular, and the polka medley
   became a staple of all but one of Yankovic's future albums.^[619][98]
   Yankovic said that "fans would be rioting in the streets, I think, if I
   didn't do a polka medley."^[620][99] More current polka medleys feature
   songs that Yankovic had wanted to parody but which had proved
   difficult, such as [621]Daft Punk's "[622]Get Lucky", which lacked
   sufficient lyrics to parody.^[623][98] The polkas are recorded in
   studio, including the sound effects which are performed live during
   recording, which Yankovic considered one of his favorite parts of
   recording.^[624][98] In 2018, Yankovic created a medley of songs from
   the musical [625]Hamilton, "The Hamilton Polka".

Original songs

   Yankovic has recorded numerous original humorous songs, such as
   "[626]You Don't Love Me Anymore" and "[627]One More Minute".^[628][11]
   Many of these songs are style [629]pastiches of specific bands with
   allusions to specific songs. For example, "First World Problems" from
   Mandatory Fun is a style take on [630]Pixies, with the opening stanza
   reminiscent of Pixies' "[631]Debaser".^[632][100] Other style parodies
   includes those of [633]Rage Against the Machine with "[634]I'll Sue Ya"
   (which features many aspects of the hit song "[635]Killing in the
   Name"), [636]Devo with "[637]Dare to Be Stupid", [638]The B-52's with
   "Mr. Popeil", [639]Talking Heads with "Dog Eat Dog", [640]Frank Zappa
   with "Genius in France", [641]Nine Inch Nails with "Germs", and
   [642]Queen with "Ringtone".^[643][101] Some songs are pastiches of an
   overall [644]genre of music, rather than a specific band (for example,
   [645]country music with "Good Enough For Now", [646]charity records
   with "[647]Don't Download This Song" and college fight songs with
   "Sports Song"). Yankovic stated that he does not have any unreleased
   original songs, instead coming up and committing to the song ideas he
   arrives at for his albums and other releases.^[648][102]

   Yankovic has contributed original songs to several films ("This Is the
   Life" from [649]Johnny Dangerously; "[650]Polkamon" from the film
   [651]Pokémon: The Movie 2000; and a parody of the [652]James Bond title
   sequence in [653]Spy Hard), in addition to his own film, UHF. Other
   songs of his have appeared in films or television series as well, such
   as "[654]Dare to Be Stupid" in [655]The Transformers: The Movie. In
   2017, Yankovic made a guest appearance on [656]Last Week Tonight with
   John Oliver, performing a new song "The North Korea Polka", as part of
   an episode about the political state of [657]North Korea.

Recurring themes

   One of Yankovic's recurring jokes involves the number [658]27. It is
   mentioned in the lyrics of several songs, and seen on the covers for
   [659]Running with Scissors, Poodle Hat^[660][103] and [661]Straight
   Outta Lynwood. He had originally just pulled the number 27 as a random
   figure to use in filling out lyrics, but as his fans started to notice
   the reuse of the number after the first few times, he began to
   purposely drop references to 27 within his lyrics, videos, and album
   covers. He explains that "It's just a number I started using that
   people started attaching a lot of importance to."^[662][104] Other
   recurring jokes revolve around the names Bob (the Al TV interviews
   often mention the name,^[663][105] [664]David Bowe's character in UHF
   is named Bob, and a song called "[665]Bob", done in the style of
   [666]Bob Dylan, is featured on Poodle Hat), Frank (e.g. "Frank's 2000"
   TV"), and the surname "Finkelstein" (e.g. the music video for "[667]I
   Lost on Jeopardy", or [668]Fran Drescher's character, Pamela
   Finkelstein, in UHF). A number of songs use the phrase "internal
   organs". In addition, a [669]hamster called [670]Harvey the Wonder
   Hamster is a recurring character in The Weird Al Show and the Al TV
   specials, as well as the subject of an original song on
   [671]Alapalooza. Other recurring jokes include Yankovic borrowing or
   being owed $5. In a number of Al TV interviews, he often asks if he can
   borrow $5, being turned down every time. This motif also occurs in "Why
   Does This Always Happen to Me?", in which his deceased friend owes him
   $5. Another recurring joke is his attraction to female nostrils or
   nostrils in general. This also appears in numerous Al TV interviews as
   well as in several of his songs (such as "[672]Albuquerque" and
   "[673]Wanna B Ur Lovr"). Yankovic also asks his celebrity guests if
   they could "shave his back for a nickel". This also appears in the song
   "Albuquerque". Yankovic has also put two [674]backmasking messages into
   his songs. The first, in "Nature Trail to Hell", said "Satan Eats
   [675]Cheez Whiz"; the second, in "I Remember Larry", said "Wow, you
   must have an awful lot of free time on your hands."^[676][106]

Music videos

   While Yankovic's musical parodies generally do not include references
   to the songs or the artists of the original songs, Yankovic's music
   videos will sometimes parody the original song's music video in whole
   or in part.^[677][107] Most notably, the video for "Smells Like
   Nirvana" uses an extremely similar set to Nirvana's "Smells Like Teen
   Spirit", including using several of the same actors. This video
   contended with "Smells like Teen Spirit" at the 1992 [678]MTV Video
   Music Awards for Best Male Video. Other videos that draw directly from
   those of the original song include "[679]Eat It", "[680]Fat",
   "[681]Money for Nothing/Beverly Hillbillies*", "[682]Bedrock Anthem",
   "[683]Headline News", "[684]It's All About the Pentiums", "Amish
   Paradise", "[685]Like a Surgeon", and "White & Nerdy". The video for
   "[686]Dare to Be Stupid" is, as stated by Yankovic, a style parody in
   general of [687]Devo videos.^[688][108]

   Several videos have included appearances by notable celebrities in
   addition to Yankovic and his band. [689]Dr. Demento appeared in several
   of Yankovic's earlier videos, such as "[690]I Love Rocky Road" and
   "[691]Ricky". Actor [692]Dick Van Patten is featured in both "Smells
   Like Nirvana" and "Bedrock Anthem"; [693]Drew Carey, [694]Emo Philips
   and [695]Phil LaMarr appeared in "It's All About the Pentiums";
   [696]Keegan-Michael Key, [697]Jordan Peele, [698]Donny Osmond,
   [699]Judy Tenuta and [700]Seth Green appeared in "White & Nerdy"; and
   [701]Ruth Buzzi and [702]Pat Boone appeared in "Gump". The video for
   "[703]I Lost on Jeopardy" includes an appearance by [704]Greg Kihn, the
   artist whose song, "Jeopardy", was being parodied, along with [705]Don
   Pardo and [706]Art Fleming, Jeopardy's original announcer and host, as
   themselves. [707]Florence Henderson plays an Amish seductress in "Amish
   Paradise".

   While most videos that Yankovic creates are aired on music channels
   such as MTV and [708]VH1, Yankovic worked with animation artists to
   create music videos for release with extended content albums. The
   [709]DualDisc version of Straight Outta Lynwood features six videos set
   to songs from the release, including videos created by [710]Bill
   Plympton and [711]John Kricfalusi; one video, "Weasel Stomping Day" was
   created by the producers of the show [712]Robot Chicken, and aired as a
   segment of that program. For the 2010 Alpocalypse, Yankovic produced
   videos for every song; four of those were previously released for each
   of the songs on the EP [713]Internet Leaks, with the videos for the
   remaining songs released via social media sites and included in the
   deluxe edition of Alpocalypse. These live-action and animated videos
   were produced by both previous collaborators such as Plympton for
   "TMZ",^[714][49] video content providers like [715]Jib-Jab and
   [716]SuperNews!, and other directors and animators.

   To help promote his 2014 album Mandatory Fun in social media circles,
   Yankovic produced eight music videos for the album releasing them over
   eight consecutive days with release of the album, believing it "would
   make an impact because people would be talking about the album all week
   long".^[717][109]^[718][110] RCA Records opted not to fund production
   of any of these videos, and Yankovic turned to various social media
   portals including [719]Funny or Die and [720]CollegeHumor which he had
   worked with in the past; these sites helped to cover the production
   cost of the videos with Yankovic foregoing any ad video revenue. He
   chose to distribute the videos to different portals to avoid burdening
   any single one with all of the costs and work needed to produce them.
   This approach proved to be successful, as the total collection of
   videos had acquired more than 20 million views in the first
   week.^[721][111] This release strategy was considered by [722]The
   Atlantic as a "web-enabled precision video delivery operation, and
   evidence of some serious digital distributional forethought" as it
   allows the videos to be seen by different sets of audiences for each
   site.^[723][112] The approach was considered to be essential to
   promoting Mandatory Fun to reach the No. 1 position on the Billboard
   charts on its debut week.^[724][57] [725]Businessweek attributed the
   sales success of Mandatory Fun to the viral music video
   campaign.^[726][113] [727]ABC World News elaborated that Yankovic's
   success is in part due to the Internet's interest in viral and humorous
   videos catching up with what Yankovic has been doing for his entire
   career.^[728][114] Yankovic himself was amazed with the response he got
   from the album and video releases, stating that "I've been doing the
   same thing for 30 years and all of a sudden I'm having the best week of
   my life"^[729][114] and that he "kind of stumbled on my formula for the
   future".^[730][111]

Reactions from original artists

   Under the "[731]fair use" provision of U.S. [732]copyright law,
   affirmed by the [733]United States Supreme Court in the 1994 case
   [734]Campbell v. Acuff-Rose Music, Inc., artists such as Yankovic do
   not need permission to record a parody.^[735][115] However, as a
   personal rule and as a means of maintaining good relationships,
   Yankovic has always sought permission from the original artist before
   commercially releasing a parody.^[736][115]^[737][98] Yankovic stated
   of these efforts: "I don't want to hurt anybody's feelings. I don't
   want to be embroiled in any nastiness. That's not how I live my life. I
   like everybody to be in on the joke and be happy for my success. I take
   pains not to burn bridges."^[738][26] The communications are typically
   handled by his manager Jay Levey, but at times Yankovic has asked the
   artist directly, such as flying to [739]Denver, Colorado, to attend an
   [740]Iggy Azalea concert and speak to her personally about parodying
   her song "[741]Fancy".^[742][116] He claims that only about two to
   three percent of the artists he approaches for permission deny his
   requests.^[743][117]

   Separately, Yankovic needs to negotiate for [744]royalties to the
   original artists for including their songs within a polka medley, which
   is considered a cover in copyright law. This created difficulties in
   recording his first medley "Polkas on 45" since it involved thirteen
   different royalty schemes, but since then he has established a
   relationship with most large music publishers to easily secure the
   license to use their songs.^[745][98]

Positive

   [746][170px-Yankovic-TheSagaBegins.jpg] Yankovic performing "[747]The
   Saga Begins" in [748]Auckland, New Zealand, on March 10, 2007. Both
   [749]Don McLean and [750]George Lucas have reportedly expressed
   approval of the parody.

   Many artists parodied by Yankovic have considered this as a rite of
   passage to show they have made it in the music
   industry.^[751][117]^[752][26]

   [753]Michael Jackson was a big fan of Yankovic, and Yankovic claimed
   Jackson "had always been very supportive" of his work.^[754][117]
   Jackson twice allowed him to parody his songs ("[755]Beat It" and
   "[756]Bad" became "[757]Eat It" and "[758]Fat", respectively). When
   Jackson granted Yankovic permission to do "Fat", Jackson allowed him to
   use the same set built for his own "[759]Badder" video from the
   [760]Moonwalker film.^[761][118] Yankovic said that Jackson's support
   helped to gain approval from other artists he wanted to
   parody.^[762][118] Though Jackson allowed "Eat It" and "Fat", he
   requested that Yankovic not record a parody of "[763]Black or White",
   titled "Snack All Night", because he felt the message was too
   important. This refusal, coming shortly after the commercial failure of
   Yankovic's film UHF in theaters, had initially set Yankovic back; he
   later recognized this as a critical time as, while searching for new
   parodies, he came across Nirvana, leading to a revitalization of his
   career with "Smells Like Nirvana".^[764][117] Yankovic has performed a
   concert-only parody "Snack All Night" in some of his live
   shows.^[765][119] Yankovic was one of several celebrities who appeared
   in the 1989 music video for Jackson's song "[766]Liberian
   Girl".^[767][120]

   [768]Dave Grohl of Nirvana said that the band felt they had "made it"
   after Yankovic recorded "Smells Like Nirvana", a parody of the
   [769]grunge band's smash hit, "Smells Like Teen Spirit".^[770][11] On
   his Behind the Music special, Yankovic stated that when he called
   Nirvana frontman [771]Kurt Cobain to ask if he could parody the song,
   Cobain gave him permission, then paused and asked, "Um... it's not
   gonna be about food, is it?" Yankovic responded with, "No, it'll be
   about how no one can understand your lyrics." According to Nirvana
   bassist [772]Krist Novoselic interviewed for Behind the Music, when the
   band saw the video of the song, they were laughing hysterically.
   Additionally, Cobain described Yankovic as "America's modern pop-rock
   genious [sic]" in his posthumously released personal
   notebook.^[773][40]^[774][121]

   [775]Mark Knopfler approved Yankovic's parody of the [776]Dire Straits
   song "[777]Money for Nothing" for use in the film UHF on the provision
   that Knopfler himself be allowed to play lead guitar on the parody
   which was later titled "[778]Money for Nothing/Beverly
   Hillbillies*".^[779][122] Yankovic commented on the legal complications
   of the parody in the DVD audio commentary for UHF, explaining "We had
   to name that song 'Money for Nothing "slash" Beverly Hillbillies
   "asterisk"' because the lawyers told us that had to be the name. Those
   wacky lawyers! What ya gonna do?"^[780][123] The [781]Permanent Record:
   Al in the Box booklet referred to the song's "compound fracture of a
   title".^[782][15] When a fan asked about the song's title, Yankovic
   shared his feelings on the title, replying "That incredibly stupid name
   is what the lawyers insisted that the parody be listed as. I'm not sure
   why, and I've obviously never been very happy about it."^[783][124]

   [784]The Presidents of the United States of America were so pleased
   with "Gump", Yankovic's parody of their song "Lump", that they ended
   the song with his last line instead of their own ("And that's all I
   have to say about that") on the live recording of "Lump" featured on
   the compilation album [785]Pure Frosting. In 2008, Yankovic directed
   the music video for their song "Mixed Up S.O.B."

   [786]Don McLean was reportedly pleased with "The Saga Begins", a parody
   of "[787]American Pie", and told Yankovic that the parody's lyrics
   sometimes enter his mind during live performances.^[788][125] His
   parody not only replicates the music from the original [789]Don McLean
   song, but it replicates the multi-layered rhyming structure in the
   verses and chorus. Additionally, [790]George Lucas loved the song and a
   [791]Lucasfilm representative told Yankovic, "You should have seen the
   smile on his face."^[792][126]

   [793]Chamillionaire was also very pleased, even putting Yankovic's
   parody "White & Nerdy" (a parody of "[794]Ridin'") on his official
   [795]MySpace page before it was on Yankovic's own page. Chamillionaire
   stated in an interview, "He's actually [796]rapping pretty good on it,
   it's crazy [...] I didn't know he could rap like that. It's really an
   honor when he does that. [...] Weird Al is not gonna do a parody of
   your song if you're not doing it big."^[797][127] In September 2007,
   Chamillionaire credited "White & Nerdy" for his recent Grammy win,
   stating "That parody was the reason I won the Grammy, because it made
   the record so big it was undeniable. It was so big overseas that people
   were telling me they had heard my version of Weird Al's
   song."^[798][128]

   In 2011, Yankovic was initially denied permission to parody [799]Lady
   Gaga's "[800]Born This Way" for his song "Perform This Way" for release
   on a new album, but through his release of the song on YouTube and
   subsequent spread via Twitter, Lady Gaga and her staff asserted that
   her manager had made the decision without her input, and Gaga herself
   gave Yankovic permission to proceed with the parody's
   release.^[801][117]^[802][129] Gaga considered herself "a huge Weird Al
   fan",^[803][130] and she stated that the parody was a "rite of passage"
   for her musical career and considered the song "very
   empowering".^[804][131]

   Yankovic states that his style parodies have also been met with
   positive remarks by the original artist. He noted that his friends and
   fellow musicians [805]Ben Folds and [806]Taylor Hanson helped to
   support their respective style parodies "Why Does This Always Happen To
   Me?" and "If That Isn't Love". He also noted positive reactions he got
   through friends his band members have, such as from [807]Frank Black of
   [808]Pixies for "First World Problems" and [809]Southern Culture on the
   Skids for "Lame Claim to Fame", and a similar praise when he
   encountered [810]Graham Nash of [811]Crosby, Stills, and Nash on the
   street, and was able to play his recently completed "Mission Statement"
   for him.^[812][102]

Negative

   [813][220px-Coolio.jpg] [814]Coolio initially reacted negatively to
   "[815]Amish Paradise", feeling it undermined the serious message of
   [816]the original song, but later apologized and said he considered
   Yankovic's parody "actually funny as shit".

   One of Yankovic's most controversial parodies was 1996's "Amish
   Paradise", based on "[817]Gangsta's Paradise" by hip-hop artist Coolio,
   which, in turn, was based on "[818]Pastime Paradise" by [819]Stevie
   Wonder. Reportedly, Coolio's label gave Yankovic the impression that
   Coolio had granted permission to record the parody, but Coolio
   maintained that he never did. While Coolio claimed he was upset, legal
   action never materialized, and Coolio accepted [820]royalty payments
   for the song. After this controversy, Yankovic has always made sure to
   speak directly with the artist of every song he parodied. At the
   [821]XM Satellite Radio booth at the 2006 [822]Consumer Electronics
   Show, Yankovic and Coolio made peace. On his website, Yankovic wrote of
   this event, "I don't remember what we said to each other exactly, but
   it was all very friendly. I doubt I'll be invited to Coolio's next
   birthday party, but at least I can stop wearing that bulletproof vest
   to the mall."^[823][132] In an interview in 2014, Coolio extended his
   apology for refusing his permission, stating that at the time "I was
   being cocky and shit and being stupid and I was wrong and I should've
   embraced that shit and went with it", and that he considered Yankovic's
   parody "actually funny as shit".^[824][133]

   In 1999, [825]Red Hot Chili Peppers bassist [826]Flea told Behind the
   Music that he was unimpressed and disappointed by Yankovic's 1993 song
   "Bedrock Anthem", which parodied the band's songs "[827]Under the
   Bridge" and "[828]Give It Away". He was quoted as stating, "I didn't
   think it was very good. I enjoy Weird Al's things, but I found it
   unimaginative."^[829][134]^[830][135]

Refused parodies

   On numerous occasions, [831]Prince refused Yankovic permission to
   record parodies of his songs. Yankovic had stated in interviews prior
   to Prince's death in 2016 that he had "approached him every few years
   [to] see if he's lightened up".^[832][136] Yankovic related one story
   where, before the [833]American Music Awards where he and Prince were
   assigned to sit in the same row, he got a [834]telegram from Prince's
   management company, demanding he not even make eye contact with the
   artist.^[835][117] Among parodies that Yankovic had ideas for included
   one based on "[836]Let's Go Crazy" about [837]The Beverly Hillbillies,
   "Yellow Snow" as a parody of "[838]Purple Rain", "[839]1999" as an
   [840]infomercial with a call-in number ending in −1999, and parodies of
   "[841]Kiss" and "[842]When Doves Cry".^[843][118] Despite these
   refusals, Yankovic was able to gain permission to parody the "When
   Doves Cry" video as part of his music video for the song
   "UHF".^[844][137] He was also permitted to write the song "Traffic Jam"
   from his album [845]Alapalooza, which is in the style of "Let's Go
   Crazy".^[846][138]

   [847]Led Zeppelin guitarist [848]Jimmy Page is a self-proclaimed
   Yankovic fan, but when Yankovic suggested the idea of creating a polka
   medley of Led Zeppelin songs, Page was "less than thrilled with the
   prospect, so [Yankovic] didn't pursue it".^[849][139] Yankovic was,
   however, allowed the opportunity to re-record a sample of "[850]Black
   Dog" for a segment of "[851]Trapped in the Drive-Thru".^[852][140]

   [853]Paul McCartney, also a Yankovic fan, refused Yankovic permission
   to record a parody of [854]Wings' "[855]Live and Let Die", titled
   "[856]Chicken Pot Pie", because, according to Yankovic, McCartney is "a
   strict vegetarian and he didn't want a parody that condoned the
   consumption of animal flesh".^[857][115] Though McCartney suggested
   possibly changing the parody to "Tofu Pot Pie", Yankovic, who is also a
   vegetarian, found this would not fit the lyrics he had written, which
   featured the sound of a chicken throughout the chorus. While never
   recorded for an album, Yankovic did play parts of "Chicken Pot Pie" as
   part of a larger medley in several tours during the 1990s.^[858][115]

   In 2003, Yankovic was denied permission to make a video for "[859]Couch
   Potato", his parody of [860]Eminem's "[861]Lose Yourself". Yankovic
   believes that Eminem thought that the video would be harmful to his
   image.^[862][141]

   In 2006, Yankovic gained [863]James Blunt's permission to record a
   parody of "[864]You're Beautiful". However, after Yankovic had recorded
   "[865]You're Pitiful", Blunt's label, [866]Atlantic Records, rescinded
   this permission, despite Blunt's personal approval of the
   song.^[867][117] The parody was pulled from Yankovic's [868]Straight
   Outta Lynwood because of his label's unwillingness to "go to war" with
   Atlantic. Yankovic released the song as a free download on his MySpace
   profile, as well as his official website, and plays it in concert,
   since it was not Blunt himself objecting to the parody.^[869][142]
   Yankovic referenced the incident in his video for "White & Nerdy" when
   he depicts himself vandalizing Atlantic Records' Wikipedia article.

   Yankovic was considering a complete polka medley with only [870]U2
   songs, but the band's publisher did not accept the terms.^[871][98]
   Similarly, he had included [872]Weezer's "[873]Buddy Holly" in a polka
   medley, but had to pull it when the band's publisher refused to receive
   partial rates.^[874][98]

   Yankovic had approached [875]Beck asking for permission to parody his
   song "[876]Loser", which Yankovic had created called "Schmoozer". At
   the time, Beck was just entering the music industry, and did not want
   his reputation to be seen as a one-hit wonder and refused the parody.
   Beck stated in 2022 that he wished he had given Yankovic permission,
   saying "I think it would have been an amazing video, I'm actually
   really sad it didn't happen.^[877][143]

   Yankovic had planned to make a parody based on "[878]Hedwig's Theme"
   from the [879]Harry Potter film series, but had been refused by
   [880]Warner Bros. Pictures. Yankovic said that perhaps if he did a
   parody based on a franchise, "it's usually better just to do it and ask
   for forgiveness rather than permission".^[881][144]

Live performances

   [882][220px-Atlantic_record_sucks_shirt_your_pitiful_aug_8th_2007_ohio_
   state_fair.JPG] Weird Al wearing his "Atlantic Records Sucks" shirt
   during a performance of "[883]You're Pitiful", in 2007, at the
   [884]Ohio State Fair

   Yankovic often describes his live concert performances as "a rock and
   comedy multimedia extravaganza"^[885][145] with an audience that
   "ranges from toddlers to geriatrics".^[886][96] Apart from Yankovic and
   his band performing his classic and contemporary hits, staples of
   Yankovic's live performances include a medley of parodies, many costume
   changes between songs, and a video screen on which various clips are
   played during the costume changes.^[887][145] A concert from Yankovic's
   1999 tour, "Touring with Scissors", for the [888]Running with Scissors
   album was released on VHS in 1999 and on DVD in 2000.^[889][6] Titled
   "Weird Al" Yankovic Live!, the concert was recorded at the [890]Marin
   County Civic Center in [891]San Rafael, California, on October 2,
   1999.^[892][146] For legal reasons, video clips (apart from those for
   Yankovic's own music videos) could not be shown for the home release,
   and unreleased parodies were removed from the parody medley for the
   performance.^[893][147]

   In 2003, Yankovic toured overseas for the first time. Before 2003,
   Yankovic and his band had toured only the United States and parts of
   Canada.^[894][8] Following the success of [895]Poodle Hat in Australia,
   Yankovic performed eleven shows in Australia's major capital cities and
   regional areas in October of that year.^[896][148] Yankovic returned to
   Australia and toured New Zealand for the first time in 2007 to support
   the [897]Straight Outta Lynwood album. On September 8, 2007, Yankovic
   performed his 1,000th live show at [898]Idaho Falls, Idaho.^[899][8]

   Yankovic has invited members of the [900]501st Legion on stage during
   performances of his Star Wars-themed songs "[901]Yoda" and "The Saga
   Begins", recruiting members of local garrisons (club chapters) while on
   tour. In appreciation, the 501st inducted Yankovic as a "Friend of the
   Legion" in September 2007.^[902][149]

   He performed his first ever European mini-tour, including an appearance
   at the [903]All Tomorrow's Parties music festival in [904]Minehead,
   England in December 2010. Yankovic was picked to perform by the
   Canadian band [905]Godspeed You! Black Emperor, who curated the
   festival's lineup. Yankovic played three other dates in the UK around
   his festival appearance before performing a single date in the
   Netherlands.^[906][150]

   A second concert film, [907]"Weird Al" Yankovic Live!: The Alpocalypse
   Tour, aired on [908]Comedy Central on October 1, 2011, and was released
   on Blu-ray and DVD three days later. The concert was filmed at
   [909]Massey Hall in Toronto, Canada, during Yankovic's tour supporting
   the album Alpocalypse. As before, video clips (apart from those for his
   own videos) and unreleased songs were edited out for legal reasons.

   Yankovic performed [910]George Harrison's "[911]What Is Life" at the
   live-recorded [912]George Fest (Los Angeles, 2014). DVD and Blu-Ray CD
   combos of the concert honoring Harrison became available in early 2016.

   Following the release of Mandatory Fun, Yankovic toured across the
   United States, Canada, and selected overseas venues in the
   "[913]Mandatory World Tour" from 2015 through 2016, principally
   featuring songs from this album. After taking a year off, Yankovic
   returned to tour in the United States and Canada from February to June
   2018 in "[914]The Ridiculously Self-Indulgent, Ill-Advised Vanity
   Tour". On this tour, he performed mostly original songs (not parodies)
   and did not use costumes, props, or video screens. Comedian [915]Emo
   Philips was the opening act.^[916][151]^[917][152] A further staple of
   this tour was Yankovic's cover performance of a different famous song
   at each venue, which Yankovic stated was something he and his band
   enjoyed doing.^[918][153]

   Starting in June 2019, Yankovic went on his "Strings Attached Tour",
   where he performed every show backed by a forty-one piece orchestra
   assembled from local musicians.^[919][154] The tour was inspired by a
   2016 performance he did with the [920]Hollywood Bowl Orchestra, which
   he considered a "religious experience" and sought to replicate on
   tour.^[921][155] The shows were generally much shorter, as under union
   rules Yankovic could only perform 90 minutes per show with an
   orchestra, requiring him to select songs that he felt would be ones
   that he had either long wanted perform to with an orchestra, such as
   the deep-cut "Harvey the Wonder Hamster" from Alapalooza, or that fit
   best with the orchestra backing.^[922][155]^[923][156] Yankovic had the
   shows open with the orchestra performing a few instrumental themes,
   seemingly giving the concert a high-brow quality, before he and his
   band entered and played his songs backed by the orchestra. The concerts
   finished with a large flashy production of his [924]Star Wars songs,
   including "The Saga Begins" and "[925]Yoda".^[926][157]^[927][158]

   Yankovic toured again in 2022, following up from his 2018 tour with the
   "Unfortunate Return Of The Ridiculously Self-Indulgent, Ill-Advised
   Vanity Tour", focusing on his lesser-known songs. The tour included 133
   shows, concluding with Yankovic's first performance at [928]Carnegie
   Hall in October 2022. Yankovic said "I've loved doing every single
   incarnation of my live show, but honestly the Vanity tour is the most
   fun I've ever had on stage, so I've been dying to get back out there
   and torture everybody with it once again!"^[929][159]

   On September 20, 2024, Yankovic announced the "Bigger and Weirder
   Tour", with 65 dates announced on the 23rd.

Legacy and influence

   With "[930]Word Crimes" from Mandatory Fun debuting at No. 39 on the
   [931]Billboard Hot 100 in 2014, Yankovic became the third musical
   artist after Michael Jackson and Madonna to have a song in the Top 40
   of the Billboard Hot 100 over each decade since the 1980s, his other
   Top 40 songs being "Eat It", "Smells Like Nirvana", and "White &
   Nerdy".^[932][160] Since then, only [933]U2 and [934]Kenny G have also
   entered this group.^[935][161] Billboard named Yankovic #15 of the top
   100 music video artists of all time in an August 2020 compilation,
   addressing that alongside his musical fame, "his accompanying video
   parodies are a vital part of the recipe".^[936][162]

   With his four-decade career, Yankovic's work has also influenced newer
   artists. [937]Andy Samberg of the group [938]The Lonely Island
   considered Yankovic an influence during his childhood.^[939][17]
   [940]Lin-Manuel Miranda directly credits Yankovic as an influence on
   his musical [941]Hamilton.^[942][17] Television producer [943]Michael
   Schur considered that Yankovic's music represented a "deep egalitarian
   spirit of our culture" that allowed his comedy writers to reflect on
   society within his shows.^[944][17]

   Certain polka musicians (such as Nick Smyth of [945]the Dreadnoughts)
   have criticized Yankovic's use of polka as comedy, saying that it
   contributes to the "dumb Euro" stereotype of portraying Central and
   Eastern European culture as goofy or unintelligent. Smyth says that
   Yankovic has "unwittingly taught the next two generations that polka is
   just a mashup comedy style," detracting from and reversing the
   community-building role that social dance music such as polka has
   always fulfilled.^[946][163]

   In 2020, Mark Riedl, a researcher at [947]Georgia Tech, created an
   algorithm that generates lyrics to match the [948]rhyme and syllable
   schemes of preexisting songs. The algorithm was called "Weird [949]A.I.
   Yankovic" in reference to Yankovic's similar song parodies.^[950][164]

Other works

Films

UHF

   Main article: [951]UHF (film)

   In 1989, Yankovic starred in a full-length feature film called UHF,
   co-written by himself and manager Jay Levey and filmed in [952]Tulsa,
   Oklahoma. A satire of the television and film industries, also starring
   [953]Michael Richards, [954]Fran Drescher, and [955]Victoria Jackson,
   it brought floundering studio [956]Orion their highest [957]test scores
   since the film [958]RoboCop.^[959][165] However, it was unsuccessful in
   theaters due to both poor critical reception and competition from other
   summer blockbusters at the time such as [960]Indiana Jones and the Last
   Crusade, [961]Lethal Weapon 2, [962]Batman and [963]Licence to
   Kill.^[964][166] The failure of the film left Yankovic in a three-year
   slump, which was later broken by his inspiration to compose "Smells
   Like Nirvana".^[965][40]

   The film has since become a [966]cult classic;^[967][167] fans bought
   it on eBay for high prices before it came out on DVD.^[968][168]
   Yankovic occasionally shows clips from the film at his concerts (to
   which [969]MGM, the film's current owner, initially objected in the
   form of a [970]cease and desist letter).^[971][169] In an apparent
   attempt to make it more accessible to overseas audiences, where the
   term [972]UHF is used less frequently to describe TV broadcasts, the
   film was titled The Vidiot From UHF in Australia and parts of
   Europe.^[973][170]

   UHF shows the creation of Yankovic's signature food—the Twinkie Wiener
   Sandwich. The snack consists of an overturned [974]Twinkie split open
   as a makeshift bun, a [975]hot dog, and [976]Easy Cheese put together
   and dipped in milk before eating. Yankovic has stated that he has
   switched to using [977]tofu hot dogs since becoming a vegetarian, but
   still enjoys the occasional Twinkie Wiener Sandwich.^[978][171]

Weird: The Al Yankovic Story

   Main article: [979]Weird: The Al Yankovic Story

   In 2010, [980]Eric Appel produced a [981]Funny or Die trailer for
   Weird: The Al Yankovic Story, a fictional biographical film that
   parodies other films based on musicians; Yankovic (played by [982]Aaron
   Paul) is seen hiding his "weirdness" from his parents ([983]Gary Cole
   and [984]Mary Steenburgen), making it big using song parodies with the
   help of Dr. Demento ([985]Patton Oswalt), falling in and out of love
   with Madonna ([986]Olivia Wilde), and fading into alcoholism and being
   arrested, at which point his father finally admits he is "weird" as
   well. Yankovic himself plays a music producer in the
   short.^[987][48]^[988][172]^[989][173]^[990][174] Yankovic and Appel
   announced in January 2022 that they would be making a full-length
   [991]biopic of the same name based on the trailer, starring [992]Daniel
   Radcliffe as Yankovic, [993]Evan Rachel Wood as Madonna, and [994]Rainn
   Wilson as Dr. Demento.^[995][175] The film premiered at the
   [996]Toronto International Film Festival in September 2022, and was
   released on the Roku Channel in November 2022. [997]The film's
   soundtrack album includes several re-recorded parodies featured in the
   film along with an original song "Now You Know".^[998][176] Among other
   nominations and awards, the film won the [999]Best Television Movie at
   the [1000]75th Primetime Creative Arts Emmy Awards.^[1001][177]

Live television

   In 2012, Yankovic extensively featured in the [1002]sixth-season
   episode of [1003]30 Rock, "[1004]Kidnapped by Danger", in which
   [1005]Jenna Maroney desperately tries to come up with a "'Weird
   Al'-proof" song.^[1006][178] In 2014, he performed at the [1007]66th
   Primetime Emmy Awards, singing a comedic medley of songs based on the
   themes of several Emmy-nominated shows, including [1008]Mad Men and
   [1009]Game of Thrones.^[1010][179]

Animation and voice work

   See also: [1011]Star Wars Detours

   Yankovic has done voice-overs for several animated series. He appeared
   in a 2003 episode of [1012]The Simpsons, singing "The Ballad of Homer &
   Marge" (a parody of [1013]John Mellencamp's "[1014]Jack & Diane") with
   his band. The episode, "[1015]Three Gays of the Condo", in which
   [1016]Marge hires Yankovic to sing the aforementioned song to
   [1017]Homer in an attempt to reconcile their marriage, later won an
   [1018]Emmy Award for "Outstanding Animated Program (For Programming
   Less Than One Hour)". Yankovic also had a cameo in a 2008 episode,
   titled "[1019]That '90s Show", during which he records a parody of
   Homer's grunge hit "Shave Me" titled "Brain Freeze" (Homer's song,
   "Shave Me", was itself a parody of Nirvana's "[1020]Rape Me") making
   Yankovic one of only a handful of celebrities to appear twice on the
   show playing themselves.

   He appeared in the animated [1021]Adult Swim show Robot Chicken, which
   provided him with a music video for the song "Weasel Stomping
   Day".^[1022][180]^[1023][181] Yankovic is the voice for [1024]Squid Hat
   on the [1025]Cartoon Network show [1026]The Grim Adventures of Billy &
   Mandy. He is also the announcer of the cartoon's eponymous [1027]video
   game adaptation.

   Yankovic had a guest appearance voicing Wreck-Gar, a [1028]waste
   collection vehicle Transformer in the [1029]Transformers: Animated
   cartoon series;^[1030][182] previously, Yankovic's "Dare to Be Stupid"
   song was featured in the 1986 animated film [1031]The Transformers: The
   Movie, during the sequence in which Wreck-Gar was first introduced; as
   such, the song is referenced in the episode. He also plays local TV
   talent show host Uncle Muscles on several episodes of [1032]Tim and
   Eric Awesome Show, Great Job! along with other appearances on the show.
   Yankovic has also supplied the voice of one-shot character 'Petroleum
   Joe' on [1033]The Brak Show. He also voiced himself on a [1034]Back at
   the Barnyard episode, and he appeared as a [1035]ringmaster who helps
   the regular characters of [1036]Yo Gabba Gabba! organize a [1037]circus
   in a 2007 episode of the children's show.

   In 2011, Yankovic appeared as himself in the [1038]Batman: The Brave
   and the Bold episode "[1039]Bat-Mite Presents: Batman's Strangest
   Cases!"^[1040][183] In 2012, he appeared on two episodes of [1041]The
   Aquabats! Super Show!, playing two different characters as the
   superhero SuperMagic PowerMan and as the President of the United
   States. In 2014, he appeared in the [1042]fourth season [1043]My Little
   Pony: Friendship Is Magic episode "[1044]Pinkie Pride" as Cheese
   Sandwich, a rival party planner to Pinkie Pie.^[1045][184] He later
   reprised his role in the [1046]season 9 episode "The Last
   Laugh".^[1047][185]

   In 2015, Yankovic voiced the supervillain [1048]Darkseid in the
   [1049]Teen Titans Go! episode "Two Parter". He initially speaks with a
   deep, intimidating voice due to having a cold; after taking a
   [1050]lozenge, he speaks in his normal voice, and the heroes are no
   longer afraid of him. [1051]Cyborg points out that Darkseid sounds like
   Yankovic, and the villain replies that he was "a true monster" for
   "undercutting musicians by subverting their words and compromising
   their artistic integrity". Cyborg objects to this, and they
   battle.^[1052][186]

   In 2016, Yankovic appeared in two episodes of [1053]BoJack Horseman as
   Mr. Peanutbutter's brother, Captain Peanutbutter, and began portraying
   Milo Murphy in the [1054]Disney XD series [1055]Milo Murphy's
   Law.^[1056][187] Yankovic guest voiced as Papa Kotassium in a 2016
   episode of Cartoon Network's animated series, [1057]Mighty Magiswords,
   which was created by Weird Al fan, musician and accordionist Kyle
   Carrozza. Carrozza sent a FAQ to Weird Al when Carrozza was in college
   in 1999.^[1058][188]

Web media

   In 2008, Weird Al joined [1059]Michael J. Nelson as a guest on the
   [1060]RiffTrax audio commentary of [1061]Jurassic Park. On November 10,
   2009, Weird Al was a guest "internet scientist" on [1062]Rocketboom's
   "[1063]Know Your Meme" video series, in the installment on the topic of
   [1064]Auto-Tune, hosted by Jamie Wilkinson. Yankovic later appeared in
   another Funny or Die short alongside [1065]Huey Lewis which parodied
   the ax murder scene in the film [1066]American Psycho, in which
