   [1067]Christian Bale's character [1068]Patrick Bateman discusses the
   nature of Lewis's musical work before killing his
   victim.^[1069][189]^[1070][190]

   For [1071]The Nerdist Podcast, Weird Al began hosting a new comedic
   celebrity interview web series, [1072]Face to Face with 'Weird Al'
   Yankovic, on April 3, 2012. The series features Al TV-esque fake
   interviews with film stars. Al has appeared on numerous other webshows,
   including [1073]CollegeHumor, [1074]LearningTown, [1075]Some Jerk with
   a Camera, [1076]Team Unicorn, and [1077]Epic Rap Battles of History
   appearing as [1078]Sir Isaac Newton in a battle against actors
   portraying [1079]Bill Nye, the Science Guy (YouTube star [1080]Nice
   Peter), and [1081]Neil DeGrasse Tyson ([1082]Chali 2na of the group
   [1083]Jurassic 5).

   Yankovic has collobrated with the [1084]Gregory Brothers on music
   videos satirizing American [1085]presidential election debates. The
   first music video was released in October 2016, titled "Bad Hombres,
   Nasty Women," shortly after the [1086]third debate between [1087]Donald
   Trump and [1088]Hillary Clinton, with Yankovic singing between
   [1089]autotuned snippets from the candidates.^[1090][191]^[1091][192]
   Yankovic collaborated with the Gregory Brothers on a similar video,
   titled "We're All Doomed" after the [1092]first debate in the 2020
   campaign between Trump and [1093]Joe Biden.^[1094][193] In 2024,
   Yankovic and the Gregory Brothers released "Deja Vu (But Worse)" about
   the [1095]presidential debate in June 2024 between Trump and
   then-candidate Joe Biden.^[1096][194]

   In 2018, Yankovic performed the theme song for the [1097]Dropout
   animated web series Cartoon Hell.^[1098][195]

Directing

   Yankovic has directed many of his own music videos; he has directed all
   of his music videos from 1993's "Bedrock Anthem" to 2006's "White &
   Nerdy". He also directed the end sequence of 1986's "[1099]Christmas at
   Ground Zero" (an original piece juxtaposing Christmas with
   [1100]nuclear warfare) from his [1101]Polka Party! album and the title
   sequence to Spy Hard, for which he sang the title song.^[1102][196]

   Yankovic wrote, directed and starred in the short 3-D film attraction
   "Al's Brain: A 3-D Journey Through The Human Brain", a $2.5 million
   project which was sponsored by and premiered at the [1103]Orange County
   Fair in [1104]Costa Mesa, California, in 2009.^[1105][197] The project
   included a brief cameo by Sir [1106]Paul McCartney, which Yankovic
   directed during McCartney's appearance at the 2009 [1107]Coachella
   Valley Music and Arts Festival.^[1108][198] Fair CEO Steve Beazley, who
   supported the project, considered the project a success and explored
   leasing the exhibit to other fairs; the second appearance of the
   exhibit was at the 2009 [1109]Puyallup Fair in Washington.^[1110][199]

   He has also directed several videos for other artists, including
   [1111]Hanson (the [1112]Titanic sequences in "River"), [1113]The Black
   Crowes ("[1114]Only a Fool"), Ben Folds ("[1115]Rockin' the Suburbs"),
   [1116]Jeff Foxworthy ("Redneck Stomp" and "Party All Night"), [1117]Jon
   Spencer Blues Explosion ("Wail"), and [1118]The Presidents of the
   United States of America ("Mixed Up S.O.B").^[1119][196] He has cameo
   appearances in his videos for Jon Spencer Blues Explosion, Hanson (as
   the interviewer), and Ben Folds (as the producer fixing Folds' "shitty
   tracks").

   On January 25, 2010, Yankovic announced that he had signed a production
   deal with [1120]Warner Bros. to write and direct a live-action feature
   film for Cartoon Network.^[1121][200] Although Yankovic previously
   wrote the script for UHF, this was to be the first film he
   directed.^[1122][200] Yankovic stated that he would not be starring in
   the film, as Cartoon Network wanted a younger protagonist. During an
   interview on [1123]Comedy Death-Ray Radio, Yankovic revealed that
   though Cartoon Network "loved" his script, the network decided that
   they were no longer intending to produce feature films. Yankovic
   initially stated that he would instead shop the script around to other
   potential studios,^[1124][201] but in 2013 revealed that the project
   had been scrapped as "it was really geared for Cartoon Network" and
   that he had "cannibalized jokes from that script to use for other
   projects".^[1125][202]

Books

   Yankovic wrote When I Grow Up, a children's book released on February
   1, 2011, and published by [1126]HarperCollins.^[1127][203] The book
   features 8-year-old Billy presenting to his class the wide variety of
   imaginative career possibilities that he is considering. Yankovic
   stated that the idea for the book was based on his own "circuitous"
   career path.^[1128][204] The book allows Yankovic to apply the humorous
   writing style found in his music in another medium, allowing him to use
   puns and rhymes.^[1129][204] Yankovic worked with HarperCollins' editor
   Anne Hoppe—the first time that Yankovic has had an editor—and found her
   help to be a positive experience.^[1130][204] The book is illustrated
   by Wes Hargis, who, according to Yankovic, has "a childlike quality and
   a very fun quality and a very imaginative quality" that matched well
   with Yankovic's writing.^[1131][204] The book reached the No. 4
   position on [1132]The New York Times Best Seller list for Children's
   Picture Books for the week of February 20, 2011.^[1133][205]

   Yankovic also wrote a sequel to When I Grow Up, 2013's My New Teacher
   and Me!.

   Yankovic became the first guest editor for [1134]Mad magazine for their
   533rd issue, published in April 2015.^[1135][206]

   Yankovic partnered with [1136]Z2 Comics to produce The Illustrated Al:
   The Songs of "Weird Al" Yankovic, which was released on January 19,
   2023. The book takes twenty of Yankovic's songs and illustrates them in
   comic form, each by different artists. Yankovic selected artists for
   the book based on past experience in working with the artists, but
   short of proofing the final work, was otherwise hands-off for various
   editorial choices.^[1137][207] Following publication of The Illustrated
   Al, Yankovic started working with the illustrators to produce new
   videos for these older songs, the first which was released in January
   2023.^[1138][208]

Other media

   Yankovic competed on a week of [1139]Wheel of Fortune taped at
   [1140]Disney's Hollywood Studios in March 1994.^[1141][209] He also
   competed on [1142]Rock & Roll Jeopardy!

   Weird Al joined the band [1143]Hanson in their music video for
   "[1144]Thinking 'bout Somethin'" in which he plays the tambourine.

   Yankovic contributes backing vocals for the song "Time" on Ben Folds'
   album [1145]Songs for Silverman.

   Yankovic was also one of many celebrities who took part in the
   [1146]NOH8 Campaign against [1147]Proposition 8, which banned
   [1148]same-sex marriage in California.^[1149][210]

   Yankovic was approached by a beer company to endorse their product.
   Yankovic had turned it down because he believed that "a lot of my fans
   were young and impressionable".^[1150][211] Yankovic later posted on
   his Twitter account that he never regretted the decision.^[1151][212]

   In 2009, Yankovic was a special guest on an episode of [1152]G4's
   [1153]Web Soup where he came as [1154]Mark Gormley at
   first.^[1155][213]

   In 2011, Yankovic guest starred as the character "Banana Man" in an
   episode of [1156]Adventure Time. The same year, he appeared as himself
   in the [1157]How I Met Your Mother episode "[1158]Noretta".

   In 2012, he appeared as himself along with [1159]Alice Cooper,
   [1160]Bret Michaels, and [1161]Maria Menounos in [1162]The High
   Fructose Adventures of Annoying Orange for the Christmas special, and
   sung with Alice, Bret, and Orange.

   On May 31, 2014, Yankovic won the ACE Award (Amateur Cartoonist
   Extraordinaire) from the National Cartoonists Society at its awards
   banquet in San Diego.^[1163][214]

   From 2014 until 2017, Yankovic appeared as a celebrity contestant in
   eight episodes of the game show [1164]Celebrity Name Game.^[1165][215]

   On November 19, 2014, a [1166]RadioShack ad was uploaded to YouTube
   which featured Al in the role of a RadioShack employee.^[1167][216]

   In 2015, Yankovic made an appearance on an episode of [1168]The Odd
   Couple as a yoga student in the class Felix takes over for one day.

   Also in 2015, Yankovic was featured as Mad magazine's first ever guest
   editor for their 533rd issue.^[1169][217]

   In 2016, Yankovic became the bandleader on the [1170]IFC series
   [1171]Comedy Bang! Bang!, on which he had previously guest
   starred.^[1172][218]

   In the official video for [1173]Weezer's cover of "[1174]Africa"
   published in September 2018, which itself is parody of Weezer's video
   for "[1175]Undone – The Sweater Song", Yankovic stands in for
   [1176]Rivers Cuomo as vocalist and lead guitar.^[1177][219]

   Though he does not appear, Yankovic is mentioned directly by name in
   the 2021 television series [1178]Y: The Last Man adapted from the 2000s
   comic book series of the same name, which involves a post-apocalyptic
   alternative timeline where all the men of the world had died. Whereas
   the comic book had the characters reflect on the absence of the Rolling
   Stones, showrunner [1179]Eliza Clark opted to update the references for
   the show, and used Yankovic as a more modern artist that had been
   considered a great loss.^[1180][220]

Misattribution and imitators

   [1181][170px-WeirdAlLimeWire.png] A screenshot of [1182]LimeWire PRO,
   showing a large number of parodies misattributed to Yankovic, as well
   as numerous misspellings of his surname (February 2007 (2007-02))

   Songs posted to [1183]file-sharing networks are often misattributed to
   him because of their humorous subject matter. Often, his surname is
   misspelled (and thus mispronounced) as "Yankovich", among other
   variations. Much to the disdain of Yankovic, these misattributed files
   include songs that are racist, sexually explicit, or otherwise
   offensive. A young listener who had heard several of these offensive
   tracks by way of a file-sharing service confronted Yankovic online,
   threatening a boycott because of his supposedly explicit
   lyrics.^[1184][221] Quite a few of the songs, such as "[1185]Star Wars
   Cantina" by [1186]Mark Jonathan Davis (not, in a double misattribution,
   his lounge-singer character [1187]Richard Cheese), "[1188]Star Wars
   Gangsta Rap", "Yoda Smokes Weed", "Chewbacca", "[1189]The Devil Went to
   Jamaica", and several more have a [1190]Star Wars motif.^[1191][222]
   Some songs misattributed to him are not songs, but spoken skits, such
   as "[1192]Sesame Street on Crack", which is also widely misattributed
   to [1193]Adam Sandler. A list of songs frequently misattributed to
   Yankovic can be found at The Not Al Page^[1194][222] and a list of all
   commercially released songs recorded by Yankovic can be found on his
   website.^[1195][223]

   Yankovic cites these misattributions as his only real problem with
   [1196]peer-to-peer file-sharing sites:

     If you do a search for my name on any one of those sites, I
     guarantee you that about half of the songs that come up will be
     songs I had absolutely nothing to do with. That particularly bothers
     me, because I really try to do quality work, and I also try to
     maintain a more-or-less family-friendly image—and some of these
     songs that are supposedly by me are just, well, vulgar and awful. I
     truly think my reputation has suffered in a lot of people's minds
     because of all those fake Weird Al songs floating around the
     Internet.^[1197][224]

   In terms of legitimate parodies of Yankovic, the [1198]Mr. Show sketch
   "Superstar Machine" features [1199]Bob Odenkirk as the character Daffy
   "Mal" Yinkleyankle. Yankovic was impressed by the parody, and stated
   that it "zeroed in on everything that's irritating about
   me".^[1200][225]^[1201][226]

Fan-driven campaigns

   The Weird Al Star Fund was a campaign started by Yankovic's fans to get
   him a star on the [1202]Hollywood Walk of Fame. Their mission was to
   "solicit, collect, and raise the necessary money, and to compile the
   information needed for the application to nominate "Weird Al" Yankovic
   for a star on the Hollywood Walk of Fame".^[1203][227] Fans worldwide
   have sent donations to raise the US$40,000 needed for a nomination. In
   addition to the preferred method of cash donations, many methods were
   used to raise money for the cause, such as a live benefit show held
   April 11, 2006, and selling merchandise on the official website and
   [1204]eBay, including T-shirts, calendars, and cookbooks.^[1205][228]
   On May 26, 2006, the campaign hit the then-$15,000 target, just five
   days before the May 31 deadline to submit the necessary
   paperwork.^[1206][227] However, Yankovic was not included on the list
   of inductees for 2007.^[1207][229] On February 9, 2007, the Hollywood
   Chamber Of Commerce raised the price to sponsor a new star to
   $25,000.^[1208][227] Yankovic's application was resubmitted for
   consideration in 2007, but he was not included among 2008's
   inductees.^[1209][230] In December 2010, the price was raised again to
   $30,000.^[1210][227] and in 2017 the price was raised to
   $40,000.^[1211][227] The campaign raised the new target each time and
   applications continued to be submitted yearly, until The Hollywood
   Chamber of Commerce announced in June 2017 that Yankovic would receive
   a star on the Walk of Fame as one of the 2018 inductees. In an official
   induction ceremony on August 27, 2018,^[1212][231] Al received the
   2,643rd star on the Hollywood Walk of Fame. The star is located at 6914
   Hollywood Blvd, directly across the street from [1213]TCL Chinese
   Theatre. The ceremony was attended by 1,500
   fans.^[1214][232]^[1215][233]

   Similar to the Weird Al Star Fund, a second fan-driven campaign called
   "Make the Rock Hall 'Weird'" has tried to enshrine him into the
   [1216]Rock and Roll Hall of Fame in [1217]Cleveland, Ohio, for which he
   has been eligible since 2004.^[1218][234] Previous attempts to raise
   awareness for the campaign and support Yankovic's nomination included a
   petition drive from 2006 to 2007, which raised over 9000 signatures; an
   art competition in 2005; additionally, a documentary film about the
   campaign is currently being developed.^[1219][235]^[1220][236] In
   addition to these efforts, an ongoing campaign is underway in which
   supporters of Yankovic's nomination are requested to send "sincere,
   thoughtful" letters to the Rock Hall Foundation's headquarters in New
   York.^[1221][236] The Hall has not considered Yankovic for nomination
   since the campaign started in 2004.^[1222][234] A 2009 Rolling Stone
   poll named Weird Al as the top artist that should be nominated for the
   Hall of Fame, followed by [1223]Rush (who were inducted in 2013) and
   [1224]The Moody Blues (inducted in 2018) in the top ten.^[1225][237]
   According to the museum's chairperson John Sykes, while Yankovic has
   come up in various discussions on nominees, "he’s never made it close
   to the ballot" due to the artists that vote towards the
   inductees.^[1226][238]

   A smaller ongoing effort has been made by fans to have Yankovic perform
   at the halftime show of a [1227]Super Bowl game.^[1228][239] This
   inspired Yankovic to write the [1229]fight song parody "Sports Song"
   for Mandatory Fun to help round out his repertoire.^[1230][240]
   Subsequent to the success of Mandatory Fun, another fan-driven campaign
   pushed for Yankovic to headline the then-upcoming [1231]Super Bowl XLIX
   at the highlight of the artist's career, which was noticed by many
   media outlets, including [1232]CNN and [1233]Wired, though the decision
   for this selection would reside within the management of the [1234]NFL
   (who instead chose [1235]Katy Perry for that
   position).^[1236][241]^[1237][242]^[1238][243] Yankovic believed that
   he would never be selected as a standalone act for the Super Bowl, but
   could see other bands include him as a special guest if they were
   selected.^[1239][244]

Band members

   Current members
     * "Weird Al" Yankovic – lead vocals, [1316]accordion, keyboards,
       occasional live percussion (1976–present)
     * [1317]Jon "Bermuda" Schwartz – drums, percussion, backing vocals
       (1980–present)
     * [1318]Jim "Kimo" West – guitar, [1319]mandolin, backing vocals,
       occasional banjo (1983–present)
     * [1320]Steve Jay – bass, [1321]banjo, backing vocals, occasional
       live keyboards (1983–present)
     * [1322]Rubén Valtierra – piano, keyboards, backing vocals,
       occasional live percussion (1991–present)

   Former members
     * [1323]Rick Derringer – production, additional guitar, occasional
       mandolin, backing vocals (1982–1990)
